- the following reports are requested for joint debate: - a5-0045/1999 by mr menrad, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the proposal for a council decision establishing the employment committee (com (99) 0440 - c5-0173/1999 - 1999/0192 (cns)) and on the commission proposal for guidelines for the employment policies of the member states for the year 2000 (com (99) 0441 - c5-0147/1999 - 1999/0816 (cns)); - a5-0046/1999 by mr menrad, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the draft joint report on employment 1999 presented by the commission (sec{1999) 1386 - c5-0215/1999 - 1999/2139 (cos)).
- (it) mr president, we are called upon to examine the proposal to set up an employment committee. in reality, the institutions of committees are often solutions adopted precisely when we refuse to face a problem or when we do not have the capacity, the power, the legal basis to face it. i think that is the main problem we are facing, the problem of who makes the decisions on employment policy. we have already pointed out that the european parliament was almost entirely excluded from cologne; i believe that parliament should be fully involved since employment policies at european level are policies of suggestion, indication and encouragement in favour of coordination between member states. there is therefore no real effective decision-making power, which, taken together, would be, i believe, a positive point in the name of the principle of subsidiarity, but rather a rather limited power. on the other hand, an apolitical committee is in danger of playing the role of a compensation chamber which tries to remove all the tragic aspects of the debate and to find political alternatives in the name of a consensus, which is precisely the neutral and apolitical consensus which, in my view, is impossible to achieve in the field of employment. the system of committees, consultation and social dialogue must not replace parliamentary prerogatives either at european level or, of course, at national level, because social dialogue, when it exists - which in reality consists of a dialogue between the large trade unions of workers, often in the most traditional sectors of the economy, and the large enterprises - often amounts to dialogues, cooperation, consultation which lead to the exclusion of those who are, today, on the margins of european economic systems, namely the unemployed, immigrants, those who are precisely condemned to remain outside the insider system, those who are outside the guaranteed work system. there is a big gap in the employment policy guidelines and that big gap is immigration. in fact, we should, as the european union and as the member states, encourage the arrival of hundreds of thousands of people who today, from countries outside the european union, are capable of performing the jobs with the highest rate of innovation, since the training of the workforce, the current workforce in the european union, is not enough. this is a bold and active policy on employment and immigration which can help employment in the european union.

- (it) mr president, i too thank the rapporteur for the remarkable work he has done and i would like to stress that the undeniable fall in the unemployment rate from 10.1 to 9.4 is to be regarded as a positive development, as is the european union's employment policy with its four basic pillars, to which i would nevertheless add language training and the immigration problem. we also believe that it is necessary to evaluate in greater depth the establishment of bodies at community level which could effectively monitor and regulate these processes. we also believe that europe can and must do more, because the europe of the euro will only survive if we build a social europe, a europe of solidarity, a safe europe with more incisive macroeconomic policies, accompanied by structural reforms that stabilise and clean up the public finances of the states. it is true that employment policies are national policies, but it is also true that they must now be brought back to the european level because it is only through consultation and social dialogue that we will succeed in strengthening the power of the european parliament in the field of employment policy, a policy which will allow europe to finally and resolutely have a political face and not just an economic one.
- (it) mr president, on behalf of the uen group i would like to congratulate mr menrad on his report. i would also like to stress that, even if the work done does not define a community law, it is impossible to ignore how important it is that parliament should express itself on an issue considered to be a priority for the union, namely the employment issue. i also believe that mr menrad's report, with the contributions he has been so pleased to accept, is in fact the best report within the limits of this subject, the powers of parliament and the constructive modalities of europe. the problem of employment, however, is not only the estimated work that parliament can or will do, but, in a much wider context, it sometimes arises from the overlapping of powers, sometimes from the vanishing of content. the indications and assessments of parliament on this subject are certainly valuable, but in the face of such a commitment, certain aspects seem contradictory to me. yesterday we heard the council and the commission speak about the imminent launch of the macroeconomic dialogue, of which no opinion or indication will be made public - as we have been told. but it's not so much about that as it is about the spirit that emerged from it, rather random, hopeful but unwilling to coordinate. the objective announced at the launch of the single currency seems to be to build confidence in economic policies through the stability of monetary, price and fiscal policies and through wage moderation and flexibility. however, today national macroeconomic dialogues have been brought down to the regional level; the consultation process has invested the regions and the territory and their capacity for programming in the use of structural instruments and the design of local development models. however, it does not seem to me that the committee of the regions is involved in the european 'macrodialogue' or that there are any indicators to assess the actions taken by the states in creating employment policies at local level, which are often left solely to the activism and will of the social partners. in addition, the same social partners have a fundamental role to play in employment and social integration strategies and there is still no clear definition of the role they should play, particularly as ngos. but that is not all: it cannot be just about wage moderation and flexibility, because that would alter the nature and spirit of national dialogues, the maintenance of social protection and all the instruments of programming negotiated in the territory, which are sensitive not only to small and mediumsized enterprises but also to sectors which correspond more closely to the tradition and characteristics of the territory. taxation and security are not secondary; on the contrary, they are more important than moderation and flexibility. it is desirable that a more coordinated, more transparent but also more determined european model be established, where the states, in their diversity, are not merely divided into strong and weak states.
- (it) mr president, the commission proposal on the guidelines for employment policies in the year 2000 is not in substance innovative in relation to the documents which precede it. it does, however, contain some very important points, particularly with regard to information systems and policies to facilitate the transition of young people to work. it should also be stressed, as in amendments nos 2 and 3 to guidelines 1 and 2, that longterm unemployment cannot be effectively tackled by means of state subsidies. i would just like to highlight three obstacles which we still have to tackle in employment policies and which the commission proposal does not adequately address. first of all, attention is too marginal to small and mediumsized enterprises, which are not only faced with the problems of excessive social security charges, which are also typical of large industries. on the other hand, the document before us does not mention the role that an active credit policy could play in supporting enterprises - the european credit system is still a long way from the american system in this respect - and i believe that the efforts made by the european union and the member states to give it a new direction have been quite insufficient so far. finally, these documents omit one essential point: the definition of the role of the regions and local bodies in an active employment policy. if we continue to think in terms of macroeconomic programming and if we do not organise local consultation and between administrators, trade unions and entrepreneurs to define concrete development projects based on real resources, we will never tackle the employment problem at its root. in this respect, amendment no 15 to guideline no 12 is important but still insufficient.
- (it) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the problem of employment is a european issue which is not the sole responsibility of the national states. recognition of this does not mean that the member states are being deprived of their powers, but that a broader and more coherent approach to employment can be used to tackle one of the greatest problems of our time more effectively. a shared institutional balance between the union's objectives and guidelines and the free choice of the member states is therefore necessary. in this sense, the committee represents a first step forward, but it is not enough. as far as the guidelines are concerned, i would like to focus specifically on the part concerning the transition from school to work. this cannot be achieved by the merely laudable proposals contained in guidelines 7 and 8. it is important to recognise the fundamental role that schools can play in the fight against unemployment, but there is no reference to the need for schools to work within the framework of a comprehensive spatial planning characterised by the involvement of the social partners, local bodies and the business community. it is therefore necessary for the commission to refer to this aspect and to undertake to promote support programmes to encourage this prospect.

the commission. - mr president, first of all i would like to thank all those who have spoken and assure you that on behalf of the commission i am taking note of your concerns and wishes. i believe - (it) mr president, madam commissioner, in april 1999 the european parliament approved at first reading its legislative resolution on the commission proposal establishing a general framework for the information and consultation of workers in the european union. the proposal for a directive lays down a framework to fill the gaps and compensate for the shortcomings of the national and community legislation currently in force. the objectives of this proposal are: to ensure the existence of the right to regular information and consultation of workers on the economic and strategic developments of the undertaking and on decisions affecting them, in all the member states of the union; to strengthen social dialogue and the relationship of trust within the undertaking with the aim of contributing to risk prevention, the development of flexibility and the organisation of work in the context of safety, and the promotion and awareness of workers of the need for adaptation; to integrate the employment situation and development in the enterprise into information and consultation issues. the proposal also aims to ensure that workers are informed and consulted before any decision is taken which may involve substantial changes in the organization of work and contractual relations; the commission has also proposed that the council should ensure the effectiveness of this procedure by introducing specific sanctions for those who seriously breach the obligations relating to these sectors. in this connection, we have recently seen - as evidenced by certain debates in this house - how important it is to define a precise framework of reference for information and consultation. this proposal will ensure minimum standards of information and consultation to be applied throughout the union. it will therefore harmonize the fundamental rights of workers - which already exist in many member states - and will contribute to strengthening the european social dimension, which is the objective we all seek. the european parliament approved a series of amendments aimed in particular at specifying the information and consultation arrangements and drawing up a definition of them, especially as regards the time when information must be transmitted. we therefore believe that it is important that it takes place during the programming phase, so that workers can anticipate changes. it is also important to extend the scope of the directive and to ensure that it contains minimum provisions at community level, provisions from which it will not be possible to derogate except in favour of workers. the content should also be extended to cover subjects of direct concern to workers' rights and their future. the commission had approved certain amendments to this report and had expressed certain reservations about others. however - and that is the reason for this oral question - it has not yet put forward a revised proposal which clearly states its position on parliament's amendments. in addition - and i regret this, because we were expecting the finnish presidency to take this matter into consideration - commission - (en) since i took office, i have said that i intend to examine the best scientific evidence available to me and make decisions on that basis. i have a unanimous opinion from the scientific steering committee following an earlier opinion from the same committee. that's what i base my decisions on. i think this is a very transparent process. the decision is based, as i said, on the opinion of the scientific steering committee. i do not see any lack of transparency in this respect. you raised the issue of the precautionary principle. sometimes the application of the precautionary principle is not fully understood. it shall apply only where there is no scientific evidence or where the available scientific evidence is so equivocal that it cannot be relied upon and where the potential damage is irreparable and the proposed solution is proportionate, cost-effective and time-limited. it seems to me that it is not wise to compare the bse problem with the hormone problem. the precautionary principle does not apply to the question of hormones. the decision on the presence of hormones in beef is based on scientific evidence - submitted to the commission by the appropriate scientific committee before i took up this post - that one of these hormones in particular - the hormone 17-beta-estradiol - is a clear carcinogen. in the light of this evidence, it seems to me appropriate to impose the ban, just as it seems to me inappropriate to lift the ban in the light of this opinion. as regards the bse crisis, it seems to me once again that the precautionary principle is not appropriate because we have a firm opinion from the scientific steering committee. its members come from most of the member states of the european union, scientists at the top of their careers and capabilities who provide advice on the subject. that's why i'm taking their advice. in view of the scientific opinion on the matter, the precautionary principle should not be applied. as far as labelling is concerned, this whole matter is under consideration and is before parliament. the commission will have the possibility of amending the legal principles if it so wishes. you raised the question of article 36. i believe that he would be an inadequate defense in this case. it does not, as i understand it, provide for a situation in which there is a harmonized market as there is. as regards transmission, there is no evidence of bse transmission other than the identified food and maternal transmission. until such evidence has been provided, it is reasonable to rely on the fact that these are the only two routes of transmission.

- (it) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur on his excellent report, which we have all read. in my view, the millennium cycle is taking place at a particular moment in the european and, above all, international economy. in fact, globalisation has become a topic of debate on a daily basis. globalisation has led to free markets and has made it possible to achieve productive results in other economic areas. i do not mean by this that we are faced with a perfect market, and for that very reason, knowing the advantages and the deteriorations of this market, we must try - through agreements such as those proposed by the wto - to establish standards and rules to protect us all from excesses of all kinds. just think of the financial speculation that can ruin entire geographical areas, leading them to poverty or even to institutional disruption. the wto negotiating cycle is not, however, aimed at creating a supranational body, as is often feared, that would supplant international law or refuse to fluctuate the free market. it also wants, and above all, to offer us safe rules for all, while protecting certain fundamental rights such as the right to health, education or social services as part of the protection of the rights of every citizen. on the other hand, as far as the prices of goods and services are concerned, it is necessary to ask not only for a tariff reduction but also and above all for a certain level of quality of goods and services, in order to protect small and mediumsized enterprises. this will be an increasingly fundamental factor in the future and it is just as crucial to underline the great importance of agriculture in individual economies by protecting small and mediumsized enterprises through appropriate standards - the report underlines this point, but it is up to each of us to make it go further and further. if we can meet these constraints and improve the quality of life through sustainable development, both economically and environmentally, we will certainly have laid the foundations for a better society for the new millennium.
- (it) madam president, i would like to make only two comments. firstly, it has been said too often in this debate that europeans must fear international trade and the millennium cycle. international trade - europe is david ricardo's homeland - has made it possible in recent decades, thanks to a new international distribution of labour, to achieve a certain level of wealth, to achieve a certain degree of integration and to curb a poverty which, with the increase in population and in the absence of international trade, would be much more dramatic than it is today. the european parliament has been asked to give its opinion on the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment. and we want to discuss the death penalty and other issues in inappropriate contexts, like trade agreements.
- (it) mr president, the attempt of the schwaiger report to reconcile what has so far proved irreconcilable is admirable, and the equally admirable effort of the rapporteur is evident from the first recital. in this first recital it is acknowledged that multilateral relations have allowed a strong increase in world trade without substantially reducing economic inequalities in the world. it is not a question of expressing opposition, of seeking greater efficiency in the system of multilateral relations, but above all of paying the greatest possible attention to certain important aspects. the first of these is the persistence of inequalities between developing and industrialized countries, but we must not forget the social dumping that is taking place within these industrialized countries. it is an undeniable fact that the process of european convergence towards the introduction of the single currency has created opportunities for growth and development for all the member states. today, however, we are faced with a different process of convergence. this process takes place mainly in macro-territorial contexts where europe does not appear to be in a strong position. its economic characteristics - based on small and medium-sized enterprises - and its cultural and social characteristics are therefore likely to be compromised. on the social side, we do not think that social protection systems and public services, i am thinking of schools, social security systems - including health - and pension schemes, should be part of the services to be regulated. the second aspect concerns agriculture. in this area, alongside market liberalisation for developing countries, there is a call not only for the maintenance of community principles - support for environmental policy, definition of a food policy aimed at consumer protection - but also for the protection of typical national agricultural characteristics and products. the third aspect concerns investment and related rules, as well as the local conditions necessary for their implementation. the first conditions for this are tariff and tax advantages. the prerequisite should be at least the harmonization of the institutes, or even the rates. moreover, the recognized prerogative of the states with regard to this policy should even be translated into the possibility for the latter to apply this policy separately on the internal territory, otherwise the flexibility of work would continue to represent the only condition - beyond the simplification of procedures. we hope that the principles of the ilo and the relevant conventions will be accepted in this area, starting with the convention on the protection of minors at work. finally, we hope that parliament will be able to play an active role in the millennium cycle, and to this end it must first of all take note of the study carried out by the commission on the impact assumptions of the new regulatory process.
- (it) mr president, i believe that the millennium cycle is an important opportunity to promote the opening up of international markets to competition, to extend the safeguards to combat anti-competitive practices and finally to tackle the question of investment. the liberalization of world trade is a positive process. it generates a certain development and it can be hindered not only by the protectionist practices followed by the states but also by the emergence of situations of distortion of competition such as the abuse of a dominant position, cartels, dumping - such as that of the shipbuilding industry - which seriously harm europe. this is relatively new ground for the international trade organization but it is very important. the same will apply to new negotiations on another subject: the search for the opening up of national markets to foreign investment. it is important that the schwaiger report and this parliament recognize that the world trade organisation is the appropriate framework for serious investment negotiations where we will have to give priority to security and clarity of law in investment protection but also, of course, to the protection of the social, environmental and cultural laws of the states. the schwaiger report naturally places more emphasis on protection. i think it is substantially in line with the commission's proposal; it therefore expresses its absolute confidence in the work of commissioner lamy on this sensitive issue. it nevertheless takes into account that the liberalisation of international trade, even in new environments - such as investment - is particularly important for the countries of the european union and all countries interested in the sustainable development of the world economy.
- (it) mr president, the schwaiger report confirmed the european union's commitment to the creation of a permanent working forum between the international labour organisation and the world trade organisation on trade, globalisation and labour issues. this commitment by the union is the only way forward if respect for fundamental labour rights is to be combined with trade liberalisation and trade in general, without falling into selfish and hypocritical protectionism or unfair and self-destructive competition. in this respect, the union must regard the bilateral agreement signed by the united states and china to prepare for china's entry into the world trade organisation not only as an important contribution to the multilateral process but also and above all as a fundamental opportunity to verify the present and future importance of human rights in the face of the opportunities offered by a market with enormous potential, the chinese market. in this connection, it should be noted that the 174 ilo member countries - countries which, with the 1998 solemn declaration, committed themselves to respect the four categories of fundamental labour rights, even though they have not ratified the conventions - are largely members of the wto. since international organizations are such as the member states wish them to be, it is legitimate to ask the member states to be consistent in all respects. the desired entry of china into the wto - for which the european union is actively negotiating - will certainly make the organization more representative and increase its potential capacity to create a free but also regulated world trade. however, we must not make a mistake: many of the problems of human rights in china are still unresolved or are not yet being solved. we should also remember that in the past the united states adopted a policy of 'double standards' in respect of the social clause. however, it is better to try to assert these rights within international institutions than in a bilateral context, according to the convenience of the moment.

institutional system. the european union must frankly acknowledge this reality: enlargement creates the need for change; we must find a solution to these changes within the community. we must have the political courage to seek long-term solutions and to carry out the reforms that the european union so badly needs. as you will no doubt remember, at the cologne summit last june, the task of preparing the next igc was given to the next presidency, that is to say finland. technical preparations were made during the autumn at the level of the permanent representatives. we have now moved to the the weighting of votes in the council, the extension of decision-making by qualified majority and, in addition to these three points, other institutional matters which are closely linked to them. the the way in which the european parliament will participate. in connection with the preparations for the conference, the extent of the conference agenda was particularly controversial. in addition to the three themes mentioned in the cologne conclusions, various aspects were discussed, including the ec court of justice, the distribution of seats in the european parliament, flexibility and the division of the treaties into two parts. we will present in our report our own views on what, after the discussions we have had and on the basis of the opinions we have gathered, is possible and desirable. there are several points on which the bodies involved in the preparations for the conference are practically unanimous. first, whatever the scope of the igc agenda, the main thread must be enlargement and the reforms it entails. secondly, no one is questioning the fact that europe will emerge from enlargement strengthened. community decision-making and the european union's possibilities for action must not be weakened by enlargement. thirdly, all the participating bodies are willing to commit themselves to a timetable for completing the negotiations by the end of 2000, during the french presidency. reforms must be unanimous as soon as possible if enlargement is not to be delayed by internal timetable problems. i have confined myself to the changes which require an igc. it must be borne in mind, however, that institutional reforms can also be carried out without affecting the treaties. in my view, these reforms must be pursued in a coherent and effective manner in parallel with the igc. i hope that by modernising the institutions and reviewing the methods of action, we can bring the european union closer to the citizens. the finnish presidency stressed the need to improve the efficiency and transparency of the european union's action. we have made a step forward in this area during our presidency. from the beginning of its presidency, finland has implemented the recommendations of the trumpf-piris report, the application of which does not require a specific decision by the member states. we have worked to increase the transparency of the council and to improve the accessibility of documents. as a concrete measure, we have published the agenda and timetable for council and working group meetings. we also proposed that the agenda for council meetings on legislative work be published. this is what i had to say briefly about the state of preparations for the intergovernmental conference in 2000. next week i will have the opportunity to discuss more with you when i am heard by the committee on constitutional affairs on the progress of the igc preparations. we are on the verge of taking - once again - decisions of great importance for the future of the european union. the adoption of these decisions and their implementation in due course will require flexible and disinterested cooperation on the part of all the institutions. i can assure you that the council is prepared to cooperate with the european parliament to ensure the success of the next intergovernmental conference.

- (it) mr president, we are on the threshold of a new round of reforms and yet another intergovernmental conference. unlike the millennium cycle, we have not yet succeeded in arousing the interest of public opinion, non-governmental organizations and even our colleagues on this issue, which is still far from our concern. we must nevertheless take advantage of this opportunity. on the other hand, as mr dimitrakopoulos and mr leinen have pointed out in their report - counsel. - (fi) mr president, the european union is very concerned about the production of narcotics in afghanistan, the direct and indirect effects of which are felt over a very large geographical poppy fields. it took note of the recent unodc report which stated that afghanistan is now the undisputed largest producer of opium. its share in world opium production is 75 per cent. opium production in 1999 increased by more than 100 per cent over the previous year and was the highest quantitatively to date, estimated at 4 600 tonnes, as a result of a significant increase in the area under cultivation. the council set out the overall policy of the european union in the revised common position of the european union on afghanistan adopted on 25 january 1999. the main objectives with regard to afghanistan include the pursuit of lasting peace and the promotion of internal dialogue in afghanistan by supporting the leading role of the united nations, the promotion of stability and development of the entire region through peace in afghanistan and the strengthening of the fight against drugs and terrorism. because of the political instability and civil war in the country, not a single eu member state has recognised the taliban government. the common position calls on the member states to maintain contacts with all groups in afghanistan. the european union has repeatedly condemned the human rights violations that have occurred in the country, in particular the continued and systematic discrimination against women and girls, summary executions and the system based on harsh discipline and oppression. the european union considers that participation in drug control measures is difficult, given the critical political situation in afghanistan and the lack of conditions for the establishment of bilateral relations. the european union has taken note of the fact that the undp launched a pilot programme in afghanistan, which progressed slowly but steadily in 1998. the programme is based on a commitment by the uncdp to fund development in specific poppy growing areas, in return for which the taliban agreed to ban poppy growing in new areas, let the uncdp control the poppy harvest and take measures to combat drug trafficking and destroy illegal laboratories.

- (it) mr president, it would be very unfortunate if the failure of seattle meant that the processes of trade integration at world level were to stop or slow down. it would be unfortunate for the producers and workers in the most vulnerable countries and not, as we have heard, for the multinationals who would, in any case, find, as they have always done, ways of increasing industrial and commercial penetration all over the world. this would be unfortunate for consumers in european countries, especially the most vulnerable consumers whose expectations would be frustrated because they would not have a wide choice of products from all over the world at lower prices than those on national markets. it would be bad for the environment if poor countries, unable to sell their products to european and american markets, insisted on exploiting natural resources. the failure of seattle was not caused, commissioner, by the inadequacy of the wto or by the us election campaign - which nevertheless contributed to it. seattle is a failure because the political will of the major countries was to slow down, not speed up, international trade. europe, commissioner, is not to blame for this either. it has in fact loaded the agenda with issues that have nothing to do with international trade by providing an excuse for those who have an interest in pressing for the process of integration of world markets to slow down. this interest certainly had nothing to do, i repeat, with european consumers or indian or pakistani workers. mr president, commissioner, i think that europe - with its centuries-old tradition of market economy, of free trade and economic freedom, which is a freedom without epithet - must affirm that market integration and free trade is an objective in itself, which we must achieve for ourselves and for all other countries. as for the other questions, let the competent bodies take care of them!
- (it) mr president, as we have already said, the outcome of seattle was not particularly positive. the only real actors were the protesters, whose demands can be shared. but they wrongly thought that the failure of the summit could in itself represent a victory, a solution to the serious problems at stake. in reality, the problems and contradictions of the world economy have not disappeared, they are present and remain unresolved: the doubts about gmos, the exploitation of child labour, the negative social repercussions of a process of globalization that is wild and unregulated. but there is more: these objections may have served as a pretext for the official protagonists of the summit - those who were rightfully at the table - not to take full political responsibility for the negotiations, which are very difficult but very necessary, on social rights and the protection of the health of our citizens, negotiations which can no longer wait. the process is complex, but europe must rise to the challenges it faces. the wto negotiations must be resumed. europe must be clearly aware of the role it must play, it must be balanced and responsible, in order to achieve a humane globalisation, open and ready to accept changes and accelerations in the economy, but also a globalisation capable of guaranteeing the right to work and the protection of the environment and health. finally, europe must not forget to defend our specificities, not only economic but also cultural and social, because the globalisation of uniformity, levelling and exploitation must not be part of the european development model.

- (it) mr president, this directive is the first time that the european commission has taken an initiative in response to a request from parliament under its indirect legislative initiative power enshrined in the maastricht treaty. this is an important fact for the interinstitutional balance because it proves that when parliament asks the commission to act on a reasoned request in accordance with the procedures provided for in the treaty, it can find an attentive and available interlocutor. this is in line with the demands made by parliament - and by the group of the european people's party in particular - on the occasion of the inauguration of the president of the commission, mr romano prodi. i believe that mr rothley's report must be adopted as a whole because it represents an excellent compromise, the synthesis of a whole series of stimulating and innovative concepts, the result of the collaboration of the various institutional forces. i should like to speak, for example, about amendment no 4, which seeks to delete recital 26. it is clear that as soon as a legal person, in compliance with an obligation, compensates a party, it cannot be denied the right to subrogate the injured party vis-à-vis the compensation body. i should also like to draw attention to amendment no 11. there is still the problem of claims in a third country. i hope that a solution will be found which preserves the approach taken by parliament, which wants to extend the scope of the directive to these cases, and which will allay the council's concerns about respect for the principles of private international law. we all have to look at this problem, especially the rapporteur, mr rothley, and the chairman of the committee, mrs palacio. a future legislative initiative could once again affect the insurance sector, in particular in the sense of some harmonisation of the standards for compensation for personal injury, which still causes so many disparities and injustices within the european union. the spanish example could be used as a basis for work, with the necessary adaptations. i urge everyone to think carefully about this.
- (it) mr president, it is indisputable that the regulation under consideration is of paramount importance. the european union is setting out the methods and timetable for the phasing out of ozone-depleting substances, and i assure you that i am not underestimating the danger of the present situation. the call for a shorter deadline, adopted by parliament at first reading, was broadly supported by the council. nevertheless, mrs hulthén's report proposes again the amendments which were not accepted, making indiscriminate progress on the deadlines for the prohibitions on the production, marketing and use of these substances. overall, this is a welcome direction, or rather a welcome intention, in the continuation of the montreal protocol. however, it does not take into account certain facts. first, the other signatories to the protocol, including the united states, envisage a longer transition period before stopping production of hydrochlorofluorocarbons. secondly, they are, in the present state of knowledge, the only real alternative in the field of fire-fighting agents to halons, the number one enemy of the ozone layer, which are still widely used in developing countries. i am not convinced either by the rigidity we have tried to maintain in the case of methyl bromide. it is a pity that the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has not tried to find a reasonable and realistic compromise. it now seems that, in a certain way, the statement of principle, even symbolically, is more important than the concrete result. the european union must be at the forefront, as was said again this evening, but when it comes to protecting the stratospheric ozone layer, the results are not measured by the progress made in europe but by a general framework. for all these reasons, the council's common position certainly seems more balanced, even if it is not entirely satisfactory.
- (it) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to express my disappointment and bitterness. i think i am interpreting the sentiments of other members who have just arrived in this house. at second reading, these new members are unable to make any contribution to improving the content of the provisions which will then be adopted by parliament, which naturally causes deep frustration. this frustration is even deeper when, in the presence of new scientific evidence, as in the case of substances that attack the ozone layer, bureaucracy and emotionality, taking precedence over politics and science, do not make it possible to make new amendments admissible that are valid from a scientific point of view. that is why i ask all members to approve amendment no 34, which i have already tabled in committee with mr bowis and mr sacconi and which has been signed today by 32 members. the amendment we want to see adopted on the rigid and closed position of the rapporteur, mrs hulthén, and all those who blindly support her, calls for a small extension until 1 january 2004 of the ban on the marketing of hcfcs in firefighting systems. these substances have a very low ozone-depleting potential - 1200 times less than halons - and are much better tolerated by the environment than substances such as hfcs or pfcs which parliament unfortunately authorizes, despite the fact that they are more harmful because they have a high warming potential and remain in the atmosphere for six to seven thousand years. these conclusions are also those of internationally qualified scientific bodies such as the 1998 cairo conference, the parties to the montreal protocol, the 1999 ozone depletion panel and the uk department of the environment. the progressive ban on hcfcs called for in the amendment takes account, in addition to scientific reasons, of social reasons because many people are at risk of losing their jobs. that's why i'm asking for your support.

- (it) mr president, it is good that the european union is seeking to expand to other countries in order to strengthen the development of democracy and improve their economic capacity to enable them to face the world market. we repeat it for the umpteenth time: we must protect the specific features of national production within this market, and thus fight against standardization and levelling which undeniably cause a loss in the specific crops and the quality of the products. but it is equally important that parliament firmly confirms that the accession of a new country must follow the effective application in that same country of the points already defined in amsterdam. in this connection, it should be noted that the problem of dangerous and obsolete nuclear power stations is still a long-standing one and that parliament has already expressed its views on this. it is equally clear that the completion of enlargement within the time limits set does not allow for the immediate elaboration of a common foreign policy or the definition of a draft economic policy, although this is urgent, in the light, among other things, of the recovery of the dollar against the euro and the disappointing results of seattle. we therefore regret that we have to adapt our old hopes to the present requirements. the only solution seems to be to involve in a new type of alliance, within the union and before the accession of new members, countries which share the same interest in a common foreign and defence policy, in common laws which guarantee the fight against crime, drugs and uncontrolled immigration; countries that want to reclaim their common right to fight human rights violations through active interventions against barbarism and massacres. we made a choice in helsinki, we decided to create the first nucleus of a european army. this is a step in the right direction and is supported by the european parliament, which hopes that this initiative will not be limited to this first step forward. we are proposing this idea in the hope that, finally, a debate will be opened on the possibility of concluding new agreements between the subjects who accept these objectives. this idea is distinct from the notion of a multi-speed europe, but should rather be seen as a two-tiered union. we propose a political level for the present member states which will choose to make a leap forward in this area, and a second level, an economic level, for the present 15 member states and the new member states. of course, in time, any state that requests it and meets the necessary criteria will have its place at the political level. however, if we do not act quickly today and if we think of enlargement by almost doubling the number of member states without carrying out thoughtful and proven institutional reforms - we do not have time to test them now - we will destroy the dream of a political europe which can respect identities, cultures and traditions while helping to create a new model of european citizenship. one thing is certain, for some, enlargement will not help to strengthen the union but to weaken it, it will destroy the hope that the founding fathers had for europe. finally, i would ask the commission to stop proposing measures for asparagus and lupins and to try to present us with a political and economic plan before it is ridiculed.

hide our faces, to believe that we can convince the portuguese presidency which in turn can convince the other member states. we can tell each other stories. we have told each other many stories, but which is a good thing, even if we have done so, as always, too late, but we are abandoning states like albania, macedonia, croatia, when there is no reason to abandon them, since we have decided that region of war, of destruction, as is already the case today in chechnya.
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. we have not reached agreement on the tax aspects - dissatisfaction is almost unanimous - and i think that the reasons for this are broadly shared. we have almost reached general agreement on chechnya and the need not to use double standards in our foreign policy but to reaffirm the basic principles of the european union. in addition, the eu's first intervention force is generally welcomed, although there are some who are against it. but this is generally the case, not only in the european parliament but also in national parliaments, when it comes to problems which concern military aspects, whether or not we are talking about a european army. the short remark i would like to make, however, concerns the most controversial point: the intergovernmental conference. the commission was not left out of the igc decision, nor was the role of parliament increased in relation to amsterdam, although progress was less than what the commission and the president of parliament said during the helsinki debate. some progress has been made and we can look at it with confidence: two representatives of parliament will be present at all negotiating sessions and will be able to speak at the beginning of each igc meeting. in addition, each such meeting will include interviews between the president of parliament and the heads of state or government. this is a small step forward, but i can assure you - and this is the important message i want to convey - that the commission and the president of parliament shared the same view and both wanted to extend the presence of parliament itself. that is the way forward, in my view. so the path we have taken is the right one, even if the pace is extremely slow compared to what we had set out to do. but we must not dwell only on the past. this is a warning, a lesson for what we have to do in the next six months. the igc agenda is not strictly limited to pre-defined topics, it is an opportunity for the commission and parliament to cooperate in order to get the reforms we want through this igc. before the helsinki conference, the agenda did not allow for any additions. the opening of the conference is not of great importance, but it exceeds our expectations and goes beyond what we were prepared to allow before this conference. but we must learn a lesson from this in order to work together in the coming months. we must cooperate very much because this openness will make it possible to bring about the reforms we need. i would like to conclude with a remark on enlargement. as president poettering said, it is true that history will tell us whether this decision is historic or not, but it is a great decision. this decision will require a very large change in the rules that guide us, the current reference points. we're going to have to change a lot of things in our lives, our culture, our budgets, our figures, everything we do. this decision has yet to be implemented. on the other hand, it is clear that this will take years and that we will have to carry out a rigorous analysis, because we have to reassure public opinion in the various candidate countries and confront it with our reality and compare carefully and seriously the legal, economic, political and transparency problems. in short, we have begun a work that will lead us to a historic conclusion. we have not yet reached that stage, but the decisions taken in helsinki will have a huge impact on the life of this parliament and the european commission. this is another fundamental point on which we will have to work to create a virtuous circle and so that the politics and the economy of the eu and the applicant countries can evolve and develop together in the future.

have been asked to put forward proposals to suspend certain parts of the provisions of the partnership and cooperation agreement. we're working on it. we have also been asked to adopt a zero tolerance strategy in the event of violations of existing trade agreements, such as the steel agreement with russia. i will tell mr paasilinna that if we are concerned about these issues, it should be noted that 40% of russian exports go to the european union, that 3% of eu exports go to russia, and that russia has a surplus of 10 billion euros with the european union. thirdly - and this brings me closer to the subject of today's debate - we must review the technical assistance we are providing to russia. i hope that mr valdivielso de cué, mr clegg and mr piétrasanta will support the points i am going to make. the european council invited the budgetary authority to consider allocating part of the tacis 2000 budget for russia to humanitarian assistance. in general, this could shift funds from traditional technical assistance to the immediate needs of the chechen population, including an element of reconstruction. but first, we must ensure that the necessary security conditions are met so that international aid agencies can carry out an effective delivery. the european council decided that the new commitments would be strictly limited to areas of direct interest to the european union and not directly supporting the russian government, including human rights, the rule of law, civil society and nuclear safety. in all, this could reduce the tacis programme for russia by two thirds. it would therefore rise from almost 120 million to almost 40 million euros. i don't like to make this list of measures. as i said in my last meeting with parliament, these measures will also have a negative impact on the european union - a point raised by two speakers in this debate. i really wish it were different. i hope that conditions will soon be created for our relations with russia to resume their normal course. i'm firmly committed to it. but that will only happen if the russians heed the message of the european council. the assault on the chechen population must stop. political dialogue must take the place of military action and the conditions for the delivery of humanitarian aid must be met. the russians need to change their policy. we can make our discontent known and exert pressure by firmly applying the economic, financial and political measures i have just announced. but in doing so, let us not forget, as mrs krehl has said and as i said at the beginning, that the tacis programme is not just about russia but a much wider region. the proposal for a regulation presented to parliament will benefit 13 countries and nearly 300 million people. it would be wrong if our efforts to make russia realize that it must pay the price for its behaviour were to lead to the many other countries being penalized, when they are dependent on the tacis programme and we have nothing to reproach them with. i hope therefore that parliament will give a favourable opinion on this new regulation. let me answer some of the points that have been raised in this debate and in previous discussions. the new regulation is intended to be flexible, designed to respond to changing situations. i hope he learns the main lessons of the past few years. it contains the views of the members of this parliament. your extremely valuable input has helped us to design the new regulation. i am glad that you acknowledge that this new programme contains innovations such as links with partnership and cooperation agreements, differentiation by region, concentration, not based on our experience - concentration, as mr clegg has said, on a limited number of multisectoral themes - greater use of investment finance and an incentive scheme to improve the quality of projects through a competition mechanism. a particularly important aspect is the emphasis on dialogue with the national authorities of the beneficiary countries in order to ensure that the national programmes best reflect our common interests. the council discussions have made good progress. many topics, including tendering and procurement procedures, were discussed at length. we take note of your proposal, which the council accepts, but the existing procedures should still be applied before the adoption of the harmonization of procedures. the commission can accept this, reluctantly, i must say, as the only practical way to ensure that the regulation is adopted in time. however, in view of the generally accepted need for simplification and harmonization of procedures at all levels, we hope that this transitional agreement will be short-lived. the commission will shortly present proposals to amend the external assistance chapter of the financial regulation in order to provide legal support for key elements of the new harmonised procedures. at this time - although when i was a member of the house of commons in the united kingdom a few years ago we considered it still early at this time - i do not think that members will want me to elaborate on absolutely all the points raised in previous discussions on the rules of procedure. i shall concentrate on one or two points which are particularly important. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of radioactive substances. that is not our intention at all. we fully recognize the role of the budgetary authority. however, the proposed ceilings are intended to ensure the flexibility needed to develop actions in these areas while maintaining the main emphasis of tacis on structured cooperation and technical assistance to the countries concerned. we are aware that many members are concerned about the key role of ngos. it must be acknowledged that the capacity of ngos in the region is not yet as developed as we all would like. it is therefore unrealistic to think that they will be able to assume more responsibilities at this stage. in many countries there is not even a legal basis for their establishment. the priority now should therefore be to help countries develop the necessary regulatory structure. ngos in the tacis countries will of course still have the right to submit adequate funding projects under the european initiative for democracy and human rights. i fully agree that delegating responsibility for project management on the ground can make our assistance more effective. but the prerequisite for strengthening the role of eu delegations is to ensure that they have the necessary staff and other resources to assume these new responsibilities. this is something we need to put in the framework of the review of the management of external aid, which i announced earlier today. at the same time, we must avoid taking risks by delegating responsibilities to recipient countries whose authorities may not be equipped to handle them. this is probably the case in all tacis partner countries at present. i would like to thank parliament for this debate. the commission agrees with many of the ideas behind the amendments which parliament has tabled and which will be reflected in the text. for purely formal reasons i cannot accept the wording in many cases, but the commission can accept amendments nos 4, 5, 8, 22 and 32 in full, as well as parts of amendments nos 1, 14 and 19. i would like to make one final comment. after listening to the speeches this evening, most of the members of this house believe as i do that we have a historic responsibility to assume at the end of this century and the beginning of this century, which is to avoid the mistakes that have disfigured relations between europe and russia during this century, to try to develop a relationship and a strategic partnership with russia, which will lead to russia's membership of the european family. it depends on how both sides develop. we're on a two-way street. we deeply regret that russia's behaviour in the last few weeks and months in chechnya has so seriously damaged, and almost destroyed, our attempts to establish this kind of relationship. i very much hope that the council's decisions and the speeches made by this parliament today will convince russia that what it is doing in the north caucasus is foolish, in breach of the promises it has made to us and to the international community about its behaviour, and far below what one might expect of a great civilized nation. i hope that in the future we will have a relationship with russia which will allow us to resume a normal course.
the rapporteur. - (it) mr president, this recommendation invites parliament to give its assent to the council's proposal on technical harmonization in the motor vehicle sector, considering it a key factor in the development of the world market. the aim of the harmonization process is to achieve the highest level of safety and environmental protection and to improve quality, which benefits both industry and consumers. the origin of this recommendation is to be found in the agreement concluded in 1958 within the framework of the united nations economic commission for europe. this agreement was subsequently amended to a large extent and other non-european countries were able to join it. the european community is thus part of a modified agreement which now includes japan itself. however, for reasons related to the orientation of the certification system, but also to the procedure, the united states could not adopt a similar decision. to overcome these difficulties, a parallel agreement was negotiated to establish harmonized technical standards, but only by unanimity and without the signatories being required to mutually recognise certificates and authorizations. the european community thus plays an important role as an intermediary, since it is the signatory of both agreements. the rules laid down by the parallel agreement are similar to those of the eec agreement, but it provides for a specific decision-making procedure in the event that a draft regulation is not examined under the 1958 agreement. in any event, a corresponding directive, proposed by the commission and approved by parliament by the codecision procedure, remains necessary for the transposition of technical regulations into community legislation. this is how the role of parliament is ensured. the commission is expected to forward to parliament the draft regulations which are intended to be applied at world level. i would like to conclude by saying that the assent to this recommendation will enable the european community to fully adhere to the parallel agreement, confirming its leading position in harmonizing and enlarging markets.
mr president, i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposal, but i would like to say that i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposal, but i will say that i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposal. this will give a decisive boost to the competitiveness of our automobile industry on the world stage, while improving safety and environmental conditions, in the interests of the consumer. as regards mr harbour's question, i would say that, as you know, it concerns state aid schemes, which are the responsibility of mr monti. i'll brief him on the questions you've asked here. as for mr lange, you are aware that any new community legislation adopted under the geneva process must be adopted by parliament either by assent, in accordance with the 1958 agreement, or by codecision, the parallel agreement. i'm open to any discussion you might have about that. i hope that we will start to talk, informally or in committee, to find the best formula.

- the agenda calls for the statements by the council and the commission on the outcome of the fifth conference of the parties to the united nations framework convention on climate change. i give the floor to mrs hassi, representing the council.
counsel. - (fi) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, climate policy has been one of the key environmental issues during the finnish presidency. i think it's important to realize that climate policy is likely to be one of the biggest challenges facing the planet and humanity over the next century. in its resolution, the european parliament rightly notes that the commitments made so far are still insufficient to limit global warming to 1.5 degrees by the next century and that new commitments are needed to complement those already made. however, the achievement of the current objectives is already a colossal task. finland has been working hard to prepare for the fifth conference of the parties in bonn. the union was generally satisfied with the outcome of cop 5, which had made progress on a number of issues. in addition, we have agreed on an action programme which will, in my view, enable the decisions necessary for ratification of the kyoto protocol to be taken at the sixth conference of the parties to the framework convention on climate change (cop 6) to be held in the hague next autumn. at community level, and in order to integrate climate policy into other areas of policy-making, finland has in particular implemented environmental strategies in the fields of energy, transport and agriculture. it should be borne in mind that these strategies are only the first step in a process which is hoped to be sustainable in order to implement and develop environmental strategies. one of the most important decisions taken this autumn was the commitment at the helsinki summit that the community and the member states would ratify the kyoto protocol by the rio+10 meeting in 2002 and that the community would take all the necessary steps to ratify it. finland also held an in-depth dialogue with representatives of ngos and the business community. in the long term, we believe that multiple links with climate policy is the best solution. although i have said that the decisions already taken will help to achieve the expected results of the sixth conference of the parties to be held next autumn, there is still much work to be done. it is very important for the union to build a bridge between us and the developing countries, to seek solutions which are equally justified in the eyes of these countries and which are win-win solutions, which benefit both development and the environment. for developing countries, the important issues are the creation of conditions for emission reporting, the development of their general capacity to act and the promotion of technology transfer. in this task, particular attention must be paid to the position of the poorest countries. much remains to be done in developing the rules governing the kyoto mechanisms. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the environment, and has recently launched a new initiative to improve the quality of the environment. the european union must make every effort over the next year to find solutions that are as environmentally and emission-reducing as possible and that can be supported on a large scale, so that agreement can be reached on the rules governing the kyoto mechanisms and on the rules governing compliance with the convention. in my view, it is important that nuclear energy cannot be accepted as a method of application in the context of cdn and joint implementation projects. the environment ministers meeting at cop 5 had a firm opinion on this matter. naturally, however, this is a real challenge in view of the current trend of greenhouse gas emissions, which are rising when they should be falling. within the union and the member states, further measures remain necessary. the introduction of an environmental tax is one of the key points in this connection. the commission's initiatives to set up a community action programme and the proposed proposal to develop an internal system for trading emission allowances are also steps in the right direction.
- thank you, mrs hassi. the commissioner has the floor.

counsel. - (fi) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have followed this debate with great interest and i must say that i appreciate the level of knowledge that the european parliament is showing in this area. many meps illustrated the debate with problems such as floods, storms and other climate disasters, which indicate that climate change is already under way. i agree with all those speakers. i also agree with many speakers who have said that kyoto is only the first step. after the kyoto agreements, we must be able to commit to greater emission reductions in order to prevent rapid and dangerous global warming. two points are essential for the european union in this area. firstly, the measures taken by the union itself to reduce its own emissions, which is also a crucial issue for the international credibility of the european union. in this area, as many members have said, the union still has a lot of work to do. i would like to comment on the question of the energy tax. what mr wijkman said, if i remember correctly, is quite true: the quantity of fossil fuels is too large on a global scale for market mechanisms alone to limit their use. our consumption behaviour influences the climate and the energy tax, the co2 tax, is a way of making the price an eco-label. we all know how difficult it was to reach a decision on the energy tax at european union level, but the conclusions of the cologne summit, for example, acknowledge the importance of this issue. in my view, this issue must be taken very seriously in the work programme of the council of economic and financial ministers. the council's common position on the famous directive on strategic environmental impact assessment, which is a good instrument for assessing the environmental impact of various programmes and measures - and also of emissions - is a step forward, achieved at the meeting of the council of environment ministers on monday. secondly, we must make progress in the international negotiations. as i said in my preamble, the most important thing now is to build bridges to other actors. several speakers rightly stressed the need to cooperate with other actors besides the united states. that is absolutely true, but i would not portray the united states in such a dark light as might be shown by some of the speeches. i have a feeling that in the united states, too, the ways of thinking are changing. concern about climate change is a notable concern among the american public. it is also seen in the changing behaviour of the american corporate world and, in my own experience, i would say that it also influences the way politicians work. although we have no clear indication yet that the rules on the kyoto mechanisms and compliance with the convention will be good, it is still quite possible to have satisfactory arrangements in place within the timeframe. as to why the union does not ratify the kyoto protocol as soon as the sixth conference of the parties, i would say that this is not possible because we will not have a clear picture of the text ratified before the said conference. if we were to commit ourselves to ratification before the moment, it would weaken our negotiating position and our ability to impose the issues we hold dear in the negotiations. although i think ratification is very important in itself, i do not think it is possible yet. i think the european union has always led the way in this matter. at the moment, it is not as clear as it was in kyoto, for example, because we are in the middle of the work phase and are busy working out many technical details. this work is not publicly visible but it is absolutely essential.
commission - (sv) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i was most impressed by the arguments put forward by the representatives of the small islands at the bonn summit. the combined effects of climate change are threatening and have already threatened their respective countries in such a way that they know this is essential for their future and their survival. i was very impressed by the concern they expressed for the future and the eagerness with which they urged us to really put ideas into action. i also listened to an elderly man from another island, where i had the privilege of living for a short time. he said, "i think that ordinary men like us, who think about their children and grandchildren, have a greater perspective on the future than you politicians. you have to think about it. do not think only of the length of your term of office, and dare not look up and look ahead". it's also a warning. i do not want to be too optimistic, mr wijkman, and i hope i did not give the impression that i thought this would be an easy game. because this part is anything but easy. we have made climate change in accordance with an idea that is politically correct and that we find in words, in a document, at the top of the agenda, but unfortunately we can see that the trends are going in the wrong direction. that means we have to act and we have to act now, because the effects may not be seen for another 50 years, or even 100 years. i agree with a lot of what has been said here. the commission has already taken some important first steps in this area, but there is still a lot to be done. i think that if we want to be successful, we also have to give ourselves the tools and the tools that allow us to be effective. we will need more financial instruments: we must actually give the environment a value so that we can see alternatives develop. we must involve the various stakeholders in a much broader way. we will need the help and support of industry to tackle the problem and we will need the mobilisation of the respective citizens of all the member states if we are to have sufficient political power and support to take action. to answer the question about the ratification process: it is clear that the commission and the european union must continue to play a key role. however, i believe that ratification by the eu alone would be a second-hand alternative. i am saying that we must do our part to get the united states to ratify the protocol as well and that it would be best to do it together, as soon as possible in connection with cop6. i also think we must make it clear that we want to achieve a coordinated ratification by the eu. without this, we will not be able to clarify the division of tasks on which we have already agreed within the eu. we must therefore do this together in the eu and with all member states on board. we need to put more pressure on the national levels that all the member states will now develop, we need to show the way and take the lead more clearly and we need more power in the whole process. i hope that the documents we will be presenting on behalf of the commission early next year will provide this driving force in all the work to combat climate change. i would like to thank you for all the valuable and constructive comments and i can also say that i do not see how the commission could do otherwise than support the amendments and the progressive resolution which parliament has put forward.

counsel. - (fi) mr president, the gardaland project, approved in december 1997 by the board of governors of the european investment bank, was already mentioned during question time to the commission. the motivations and advantages of the project in question have already been explained in detail. in this respect, it is probably worth recalling that for all projects evaluated and prepared by the eib, respect for the environment is one of the basic conditions for approval of project financing, as was also the case for the gardaland project. as regards the transparency of documents relating to the gardaland project and other projects envisaged in the community, it is essential to understand that the eib is really a bank. the documents in its possession cannot therefore be accessed in the same way as those of the council bodies which prepare legislative texts, for example. in order to maintain the confidence of all parties, the eib may not make public any information on documents or data in its possession concerning lenders or any other party involved in the preparation or implementation of the bank's projects without the express permission of those parties. it should also be borne in mind that although the ministers appointed by the member states to the board of governors of the eib are usually the same as those who participate in the meetings of the council of ministers of economy and finance, this ecofin council is not empowered to make recommendations to the european investment bank regarding its lending policy or the related information policy. on 26 march 1997 the eib's board of governors adopted rules on the publication of documents, which were published on 9 august 1997 on page 13 of the official journal of the european communities (c 243). within the framework of these rules, the european investment bank practices the disclosure of documents and information, although the general aim must be to optimise the exchange of information and the principle of transparency, without however threatening professional secrecy. while the community grants a guarantee to the european investment bank in the european budget for the granting of certain loans, for example for projects in third countries, the council decided that the eib should send an annual statement to parliament and the council on the actions undertaken with the aid of eib loans.

- (it) mr president, the process of globalisation of our economy, which includes the completion of the single market, is made up of regional districts and energy centres which become international because they manage to form a network. to be fully realised, this process needs clear, simple and effective rules, which our businesses and our craft industries have not been able to take advantage of in a crucial sector such as commercial payments. in this sense, the council's position, presented at second reading, is still weak and, above all, it does not protect small and mediumsized enterprises and craft industries from the inefficiency of the public apparatus and the excessive power of certain large enterprises. the amendments tabled by parliament are intended to fill this serious gap and pursue the ambitious objective of creating a legislative system which, while avoiding unnecessary bureaucratic complications, allows the major productive centres of the union to express themselves in the best possible way on the internal market, which would facilitate their action on the world market. the most significant innovations we intend to introduce are: the careful examination of the consequences of late payment for perishable foodstuffs; the recognition of the right to demand reimbursement of costs resulting from the recovery of the credit; the reservation of title clause in favour of the seller; the assurance that an enforceable title can be obtained, as a rule, within 60 days of the date on which the creditor has brought an action; finally, greater transparency in relations between the contracting authority and the contracting entity and in subcontracting relations. this set of amendments can mark the beginning of a real cultural change in the present relationship between debtor and creditor. my last remark will be of a strictly political nature. at a time when there is so much talk about the role and future of the community institutions, i would like to point out that the credibility of a body such as parliament or the council is strictly linked to its ability to solve the problems that beset the daily lives of citizens or to intervene in a positive way.
